| **[The Answer Knows Where to Look: Zooming before MLLMs Answer under Visual-Token Budgets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32664v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) read images through visual tokens, and under the small per-image budgets that efficient deployment requires, the fine text of documents, charts and infographics becomes illegible. Zooming in recovers the evidence, but the region must be chosen before the model answers. We find that MLLMs know where the evidence is, but not in a form that can be read before they answer: the answer tokens localize it from the middle layers on, whereas the attention available before answering does so only in the top layers, at a depth that varies across models, and diffusely. Supervising the pre-answer attention to point degrades reading, and the benefit of zooming is governed by each image's information gap. Building on these findings, we introduce ours, which lets an MLLM look where its answer would. A dedicated look token, processed in a single prefill and kept out of the answering pass, learns in hindsight to attend as the model's own correct answers do, extracting this knowledge into a concentrated pointer before any answer exists. Its hidden state then selects how to look, from no zoom to crops, square windows, windows that adapt to the pointer and foveated warps, supervised by the counterfactual outcome of every view. ours needs no box annotations, per-model layer search, external localizers or generation before the answer. On Qwen3-VL at three scales and five text-rich benchmarks, it surpasses the strongest baseline by 4.8-9.1 points at 98 visual tokens per view and attention-guided zooming by 11.1-17.0 points, while running faster than attention-guided and grounding-based zooming.</p></details> |  |
| **[StemBind: When MLLMs Get Lost Between Rules and Instances in Abstract Visual Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.00148v2)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) often know the rule but pick the wrong answer: on abstract visual reasoning (AVR) tasks, a model can describe what it sees and name the underlying pattern, yet still fail to choose the matching candidate. Existing AVR benchmarks cannot detect this because they collapse perception, rule induction, and answer selection into a single right-or-wrong signal. We introduce StemBind, a shared-stem diagnostic benchmark that probes the same visual stem with three aligned questions: Perception (what is in the image), Rule (what pattern governs it), and Full (which option completes it), so a final-answer error can be attributed to a specific sub-step on the same evidence. StemBind contains 2,298 curated knowledge-light stems across nine auditable visual operations, totaling 19,533 P/R/F tasks, with each full item annotated by Sternberg's four reasoning stages (S1 Encode, S2 Infer, S3 Map, S4 Apply). Evaluating 24 frontier MLLM configurations yields four findings. (i) The R-F chasm: rule accuracy exceeds full-item accuracy on 22 of 24 models, so most failures happen after the rule is identified. (ii) A persistent binding gap: even when P and R are both correct on the same stem, models still answer F incorrectly 51.2% of the time. (iii) The bottleneck is S3: process diagnostics and Stage-wise Stimulus Augmentation localize the dominant failure to rule-to-instance mapping. (iv) Scaling and thinking do not help: neither larger models nor explicit thinking mode reliably closes the gap, and thinking even lowers rule and full-item accuracy. StemBind reframes AVR evaluation from final-answer ranking to locating where abstract visual reasoning breaks down, identifying rule-to-instance binding as a concrete next target for vision-grounded reasoning.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at NeurIPS 2026 (Evaluations and Datasets Track). Camera-ready version. Project page: https://hexixiang.github.io/StemBind ; Code: https://github.com/hexixiang/StemBind ; Data: https://huggingface.co/datasets/user48271/Stembind</p></details> |
| **[Making MLLM Latents Matter: Factor-Aware Predictive Learning for Cinematic Video Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30926v2)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Cinematic video generation requires both temporal inference from limited inputs (i.e., sparse rather than frame-by-frame guidance) and factor-aware control of character, scene, action, and camera. Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) offer useful priors for both capabilities, but their representations are not automatically actionable for video generation: a Video Diffusion Transformer (DiT) may bypass them through native conditioning pathways, and using them does not ensure either factor-aware control or generation-compatible temporal prediction. We propose Factor-Aware Predictive Learning (FAPL), which establishes effective MLLM latent use as a shared prerequisite and then targets the two capabilities with complementary learning signals. First, latent-only generator grounding trains the DiT with a frozen MLLM and connector, strengthening the DiT's use of MLLM latents for generation. Second, for factor-aware control, paired videos specify a target-factor change and non-target preservation, and factor-span replacement trains the DiT to realize this relationship in its output. Third, for temporal inference, text-only and text-plus-complete-video observations share a video target, and the trained DiT is then frozen while the MLLM and connector are optimized through it. This final stage trains limited-input representations for generation while retaining complete-observation reconstruction as a complementary training mode. Extensive experiments demonstrate our consistently better performance on three cinematic video generation tasks (i.e., sparse-to-video, factor-aware editing, and multi-shot generation) over baselines and validate FAPL's contributions to MLLM feature use, temporal control, and factor coordination.</p></details> |  |
| **[Beyond Pattern Matching in 3D MLLMs: Part-Level Functional Reasoning for Generalizable Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32262v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>3D MLLMs recognize objects via pattern matching, which fails when geometrically similar objects require functional reasoning to disambiguate (e.g., dresser vs. wardrobe). Our per-sample Geometric Confusion Score (GCS) exposes this failure: baseline accuracy is 33.7% on ModelNet40's most ambiguous 20% versus 84.0% on its least ambiguous 20%. We identify a function-as-annotation pattern, in which functional language decorates a known identity instead of supporting inference, and hypothesize that reversing this information flow should concentrate improvements on the most ambiguous samples. PFIR (Part-level Functional Inference for Recognition) reverses that flow. Built on PartNeXt part segmentation, its data pipeline produces 507K QA pairs with functional CoTs (geometry $\to$ function $\to$ identity), and its progressive curriculum culminates in dual-objective GRPO: strict functional reasoning and descriptive grounding for Objaverse's diverse distribution. The two RL objectives yield nearly orthogonal task vectors ($\cos = 0.056$), enabling composition via Task Arithmetic. Under a strict dual OOD protocol (Objaverse $\to$ ModelNet40; XYZRGB $\to$ XYZ-only), PFIR raises zero-shot accuracy from 60.5% to 66.7%, with gains concentrated $2.6\times$ more on geometrically ambiguous samples---supporting part-level functional reasoning as an effective route to more generalizable 3D recognition. Code: https://github.com/this233/pfir-3d.</p></details> |  |
| **[OmniSpace: Efficient Geometry Awareness for Autonomous Vehicles MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.22617v3)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have achieved remarkable performance on 2D visual tasks, yet enhancing their spatial intelligence for real-world applications such as Autonomous Vehicles (AV) remains an open challenge. Existing geometry-aware MLLMs typically rely on auxiliary 3D models at inference time, introducing pipeline complexity and the risk of cascading failures. In this paper, we present OmniSpace, a simple yet effective plug-and-play paradigm for geometry-aware spatial reasoning from purely 2D observations. Motivated by our finding that current MLLMs are bottlenecked by weak cross-view correspondence and depth estimation, OmniSpace introduces a Camera Pose Injector, a Multi-view Epipolar Attention module, and a 3D Geometric Distillation objective that jointly address these two limitations by transferring geometric knowledge into the model. Extensive experiments show that OmniSpace surpasses existing methods on planning benchmarks (nuScenes, Bench2Drive), risk detection (nuInstruct), language (Omnidrive), and generalization (DriveBench).</p></details> |  |
| **[Place-it-R1: Unlocking Environment-aware Reasoning Potential of MLLM for Video Object Insertion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.06140v3)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Video object insertion is fundamental to video editing, yet existing diffusion methods often produce visually plausible but physically inconsistent results. We present Place-it-R1, an end-to-end framework for physically plausible video object insertion driven by environment-aware MLLM reasoning. Rather than treating reasoning as a generic text prompt, Place-it-R1 uses the MLLM to analyze the target environment, infer object-scene interactions, and determine where an insertion is physically valid. The resulting reasoning is translated into two complementary forms of guidance for video diffusion: semantic guidance that describes the intended physical interaction and spatial guidance that provides a valid insertion region in each frame. To further align generation with local physical realism, we introduce Spatial Direct Preference Optimization, which leverages an MLLM to rank generated candidates, and introduces a region-aware preference objective that explicitly localizes physical-violation penalties to the inserted-object region. Place-it-R1 further offers flexible and standard modes to trade off environment adaptation and scene preservation. Extensive experiments show that Place-it-R1 produces more physically coherent and visually natural insertions than state-of-the-art methods and achieves competitive results against commercial systems.</p></details> | <details><summary>https...</summary><p>https://nevsnev.github.io/Place-it-R1/</p></details> |
| **[Human Cognitive Benchmarks Reveal Foundational Visual Gaps in MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.16435v5)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Humans develop perception through a bottom-up hierarchy: from basic primitives and Gestalt principles to high-level semantics. Current Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) are trained directly on complex downstream tasks. Does it guarantee their performance on these foundational visual capabilities? To systematically investigate this gap, we introduce VisFacor, a benchmark that digitizes 20 vision-centric subtests from FRCT, a well-established cognitive psychology assessment spanning four domains of human visual cognition. Furthermore, we design algorithms to automatically construct and validate unlimited test cases with controllable difficulty. Using VisFacor, we evaluate 39 frontier MLLMs, including both proprietary (e.g., GPT, Gemini) and open-source (e.g., LLaMA, Qwen) models. The best model achieves a score of only 55.9%. Analysis reveals good internal consistency (Cronbach's alpha = 0.94) and construct validity (compared to existing vision benchmarks). Models consistently fail on tasks such as mental rotation, spatial relation inference, and figure-ground discrimination, regardless of model size or prompting strategy. These findings suggest that performance improvements on existing general benchmarks might represent castles in the air instead of a genuine mastery of human-like visual cognition.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to NeurIPS 2026</p></details> |
| **[PI-MLLM: Psychophysics-Inspired Multimodal Large Language Model for Oddly Satisfying Video Emotion Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28264v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Oddly Satisfying Videos (OSVs) can evoke strong viewer responses through repetitive motion, visual tactility, and ASMR-like sounds despite containing few explicit semantic emotion cues. This makes OSVs a challenging setting for Video Emotion Analysis (VEA), since most existing methods focus on semantic video content and under-model the physical sensory dynamics that shape viewer responses. To support Oddly Satisfying Video Emotion Analysis (OSVEA), we construct the Oddly Satisfying Video Dataset (OSVD), comprising 12,000 annotated 5-second clips. Each clip provides viewer-emotion labels under video-only, audio-only, and audio-visual conditions, together with explanations grounded in visual and acoustic sensory evidence. Building on OSVD, we propose PI-MLLM, a psychophysics-inspired multimodal large language model that integrates Modality-Specific Dynamics Alignment (MSDA) and Coalition-Based Audio-Visual Attribution (CAVA). MSDA uses auxiliary prediction objectives to align modality representations with visual motion and acoustic energy-onset dynamics. CAVA evaluates emotion scores across modality coalitions and applies Shapley attribution to identify the dominant modality and determine whether visual and auditory evidence is synergistic or conflicting. Experiments show that PI-MLLM improves both viewer-side emotion prediction and sensory-grounded explanation quality, suggesting the value of physical sensory cues to video emotion analysis.</p></details> | <details><summary>12 pa...</summary><p>12 pages, 5 figures, 8 tables; includes supplementary material</p></details> |
| **[Beyond Omni: A Unified MLLM for Embodied Sensory Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26751v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent embodied systems increasingly build on multimodal large language models (MLLMs) for their pretrained understanding and reasoning capabilities, but their perception of the physical world remains largely limited to basic modalities such as vision and audio. However, broadening MLLMs to more diverse sensory modalities from the physical environment is challenging: their representations are often weakly connected to the MLLM and vary substantially in structure and statistics, while their interpretation still requires shared high-level understanding and reasoning. We therefore propose Unisense, a unified embodied MLLM framework that supports diverse physical sensory modalities within a shared multimodal architecture. Unisense follows a two-stage training process: (i) aligning sensory representations with language to establish a language-accessible interface; and (ii) tuning modality-specific experts within the shared LLM to provide specialized processing for different sensory modalities. To support broad sensory learning, we curate Unisense-1M, a large-scale embodied sensory training corpus comprising 1M samples across 8 sensory modalities. We further evaluate Unisense on 12 benchmarks spanning depth, point cloud, IMU, tactile, thermal, radar, motion, and smell understanding. Across these heterogeneous sensing tasks, Unisense achieves strong performance and outperforms both generalist and specialized models on most benchmarks, demonstrating the potential of pretrained MLLMs as unified models for embodied sensory understanding.</p></details> |  |
| **[Fundus-R1: Training a Fundus-Reading MLLM on Public Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.08322v2)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Fundus imaging such as CFP, OCT and UWF is crucial for the early detection of retinal anomalies and diseases. Fundus image understanding, due to its knowledge-intensive nature, poses a challenging vision-language task. An emerging approach to addressing the task is to post-train a generic multimodal large language model (MLLM), either by supervised finetuning (SFT) or by reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR), on a considerable amount of in-house samples paired with high-quality clinical reports. However, these valuable samples are not publicly accessible, which not only hinders reproducibility but also practically limits research to few players. To overcome the barrier, we make a novel attempt to train a reasoning-enhanced fundus-reading MLLM, which we term Fundus-R1, using exclusively public datasets, wherein over 94\% of the data are annotated with only image-level labels. Our technical contributions are two-fold. First, we propose a RAG-based method for composing image-specific, knowledge-aware reasoning traces. Such auto-generated traces link visual findings identified by a generic MLLM to the image labels in terms of ophthalmic knowledge. Second, we enhance RLVR with a process reward that encourages self-consistency of the generated reasoning trace in each rollout. Extensive experiments on three fundus-reading benchmarks, i.e., FunBench, Omni-Fundus and GMAI-Fundus, show that Fundus-R1 clearly outperforms multiple baselines, including its generic counterpart (Qwen2.5-VL) and a stronger edition post-trained without using the generated traces. This work paves the way for training powerful fundus-reading MLLMs with publicly available data.</p></details> |  |
| **[Can MLLMs Find Their Way in a City? Exploring Emergent Navigation from Web-Scale Knowledge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.15933v4)** | 2026-10-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Leveraging multimodal large language models (MLLMs) to develop embodied agents offers significant promise for addressing complex real-world tasks. However, current evaluation benchmarks remain predominantly language-centric or heavily reliant on simulated environments, rarely probing the nuanced, knowledge-intensive reasoning essential for practical, real-world scenarios. To bridge this critical gap, we introduce the task of Sparsely Grounded Visual Navigation, explicitly designed to evaluate the sequential decision-making abilities of MLLMs in challenging, knowledge-intensive real-world environment. We operationalize this task with CityNav, a comprehensive benchmark encompassing four diverse global cities, specifically constructed to assess raw MLLM-driven agents in city navigation. Agents are required to rely solely on visual inputs and internal multimodal reasoning to sequentially navigate 50+ decision points without additional environmental annotations or specialized architectural modifications. Crucially, agents must autonomously achieve localization through interpreting city-specific cues and recognizing landmarks, perform spatial reasoning, and strategically plan and execute routes to their destinations. Through extensive evaluations, we demonstrate that current state-of-the-art MLLMs, reasoning techniques (e.g., GEPA, chain-of-thought, reflection) and competitive baseline PReP significantly underperform in this challenging setting. To address this, we propose Verbalization of Path(VoP), which explicitly grounds the agent's internal reasoning by probing city-scale cognitive maps (key landmarks and directions toward the destination) from the MLLM, substantially enhancing navigation success. Project Webpage: https://dwipddalal.github.io/AgentNav/</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at EACL 2026 (ORAL)</p></details> |
| **[MLLM-Guided Autonomous Object Detection for Open-World Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.10844v2)** | 2026-10-17 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Object detection typically relies on predefined category vocabularies, while open-world and open-vocabulary approaches remain limited by generic unknown labels or externally specified detection concepts. We propose LAOD, an MLLM-Guided Autonomous Object Detection framework in which a Multimodal Large Language Model (MLLM) generates an image-specific detection vocabulary that is grounded by an open-vocabulary detector. This decomposition separates semantic concept discovery from spatial localization and removes the need to manually enumerate target categories at inference time. We evaluate LAOD using Class-Agnostic Average Precision (CAAP), Semantic Naming Average Precision (SNAP), and Semantic Localization Average Precision (SemLoc-AP), which measure localization, semantic naming, and their correct association, respectively. Experiments on COCO-Val, LVIS-minival, and COCO-OOD across multiple MLLMs and detectors demonstrate strong performance across common, long-tailed, and out-of-distribution objects, while showing that grounding quality remains a major determinant of end-to-end performance. LAOD also substantially outperforms both direct MLLM-based object detection and dedicated models trained specifically for open-ended detection. Overall, the results support a modular formulation of autonomous object detection in which MLLMs determine what to detect and specialized open-vocabulary detectors determine where those concepts occur.</p></details> |  |
| **[MobileInject: Evaluating Reasoning-to-Action Security of MLLM-Based Mobile Agents under Cross-Modal Consistency Injection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23307v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal large language model (MLLM)-based mobile agents are rapidly evolving from conversational assistants into autonomous systems that perceive, reason over, and act on smartphone environments. This capability also exposes a new security risk: adversaries can embed malicious objectives in cross-application mobile content and steer agent behavior through indirect multimodal injection. Although prompt injection has been studied in desktop and web settings, its cross-modal properties in mobile ecosystems remain poorly understood, and existing security analyses are still limited. To close this gap, we introduce MobileInject, the first benchmark for evaluating mobile agents against multimodal injection attacks and reasoning-to-action security risks. MobileInject provides a high-fidelity interactive mobile evaluation framework covering 11 scenarios, 9 security-risk categories, and 108 executable risk instances. It further introduces Cross-Modal Consistency Injection to exploit corroboration across modalities, two enhanced attack methods, Multi-Role Collaborative Deception and Reasoning-Camouflage Injection, to intensify trust manipulation and reasoning hijacking, and a Reasoning Security Diagnosis Framework for fine-grained failure analysis. Systematic experiments show that mobile agents are broadly vulnerable to multimodal injection, and that many failures stem from reasoning hijacking at early stages. These findings establish MobileInject as a systematic cross-modal benchmark for studying and improving the security of mobile agents. Code release details are provided in the Appendix Open Science section.</p></details> |  |
| **[MLLM Frame Subset Ensembling for Audio-Visual Video QA and MLLM-based Reranking for Ad-hoc Video Search in TRECVID 2025](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23214v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>This paper presents the overview of the runs related to the Ad-hoc Video Search (AVS) and Video Question Answering (VQA) tracks of TRECVID 2025 on behalf of the CERTH-ITI team. For the AVS track, we introduce a two-stage framework built on foundation models. In the first stage, multiple vision-language models (VLMs) encode both the input query, augmented through LLM-generated rephrasings, and the candidate video shots, producing weighted similarity scores for initial retrieval. In the second stage, we utilize a Multimodal-LLM(MLLM)-based reranking module that evaluates the semantic alignment between each shot among the top-N highest-ranked ones and the original query, generating updated relevance scores for reordering these shots. This MLLM-driven reranking significantly improves contextual matching and produces more accurate final rankings without requiring any model training. Regarding the VQA track, we fine-tune an audio-visual MLLM model on the provided TRECVID training dataset and we implement an inference-time scaling technique to enhance the multimodal understanding capabilities of the MLLM. For the open-ended Answer Generation (AG) task, we aggregate multiple model responses per question via a majority vote. The responses are generated with greedy sampling from different random frame subsets of the video and they are ranked based on the number of votes. For the Multiple-Choice (MC) task, instead of voting, we use mean pooling on the logits assigned by the fine-tuned model to each candidate response. Through the combination of fine-tuning and frame subset ensembling we achieve the highest score across 3 metrics in the VQA AG task and the second highest in the VQA MC task.</p></details> | <details><summary>Proc....</summary><p>Proc. TREC 2025 Workshop, Dec. 2025</p></details> |
| **[LDDR: Linear-DPP Dynamic-Resolution Frame Sampling for Video MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.11477v2)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal large language models, when used for long video understanding, require selecting an informative frame set from massive and redundant candidates. Limited by the model's visual-token budget and reasoning ability, frame selection is therefore essential to reduce input size while preserving the most relevant information for accurate video reasoning. Existing frame-sampling methods like uniform sampling or relevance/diversity-sampling often miss important events or retain redundant frames. On the other hand, agentic frame selection methods incur very high computational costs. Further, these methods allocate equal tokens to all selected frames, regardless of their importance. We therefore propose LDDR (Linear DPP Dynamic Resolution), which is a training-free and budget-aware video frame sampling framework. It is computationally efficient and allows non-uniform token budget allocation within the candidate frame set. Specifically, LDDR performs query-aware Determinantal Point Process (DPP) frame selection in a task-conditioned feature space, achieving a 3x runtime speedup over standard DPP baselines. It further introduces a Group DPP importance score to guide frame retention and dynamic resolution allocation: assigning more tokens to informative and non-redundant frames while downscaling or pruning less useful ones. We test LDDR on four popular video benchmarks spanning short, medium, and long-range videos. LDDR achieves a 2.5-point gain in low-budget settings and a 1.6-point gain in high-budget scenarios. These improvements are consistently observed across multiple MLLM families and sizes. Further manual qualitative analyses are included to prove the method's effectiveness.</p></details> | 21 pages, 4 figures |
| **[PACE: Pareto-Adaptive Compression for Efficient Native MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24005v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Native multimodal large language models (native MLLMs) jointly encode visual and linguistic sequences within a unified autoregressive Transformer, emerging as a promising paradigm for multimodal understanding. Unlike conventional ViT-based MLLMs relying on external vision encoders, ViT-free native MLLMs tokenize pixel patches directly, potentially suffering severe visual token redundancy. A pilot study confirms this: unlike ViT-based models that degrade under compression, ViT-free MLLMs tolerate aggressive compression with near-lossless or even improved performance. Yet existing compression methods target ViT-based settings, leaving native MLLMs, which stand to gain the most, unexplored. To close this gap, we introduce an analysis pipeline centered on token discriminability and cross-dimensional heterogeneous redundancy, revealing that (i) redundancy is distributed independently and heterogeneously along height and width axes, and (ii) early-layer visual tokens are indiscriminable and uniformly redundant. Guided by these findings, we propose PACE (Pareto-Adaptive Compression for Efficient Native MLLMs), the first visual token compression framework tailored for native MLLMs with Multimodal-RoPE. Leveraging dimensional decoupling of positional encoding, PACE decomposes attention into two orthogonal spatial saliency estimates along height and width, gated by a positional attention signal, yielding direction-aware importance with negligible overhead. It further casts token budget allocation as Pareto optimization over performance preservation, inference efficiency, and spatial coverage, jointly identifying optimal compression depths and retention ratios. Experiments show PACE maintains near-lossless performance at only 25%-50% visual token retention, even improving several benchmarks, while delivering over 2x FLOPs reduction.</p></details> | <details><summary>NeurI...</summary><p>NeurIPS 2026 Accepted Paper</p></details> |
| **[Can MLLMs Read the Room? A Multimodal Benchmark for Assessing Deception in Multi-Party Social Interactions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.16221v2)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Despite their advanced reasoning capabilities, state-of-the-art Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) still lack a core component of human intelligence: the ability to "read the room" and assess deception in complex social interactions. To rigorously evaluate this limitation, we introduce Multimodal Interactive Deception Assessment (MIDA), a new task and multimodal dataset comprising synchronized video and text with verifiable ground-truth labels for every statement. We benchmark 12 state-of-the-art open- and closed-source MLLMs and identify a substantial performance gap: even powerful models such as GPT-4o struggle to reliably distinguish truthful statements from deceptive ones. Our analysis shows that current models have difficulty grounding language in multimodal social cues and modeling what others know, believe, or intend. To address these limitations, we propose a Social Chain-of-Thought (SoCoT) reasoning pipeline and a Dynamic Social Epistemic Memory (DSEM) module. Experimental results show that structured epistemic memory consistently improves deception assessment, while multimodal social-cue reasoning remains a challenging and important direction for future research.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to EMNLP 2026</p></details> |
| **[Seeing is Believing: Benchmarking and Mitigating the Interference of Language Priors on Visual Perception in MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21902v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Language priors in MLLMs can override visual evidence, impairing visual perception. Existing benchmarks assume shared priors across models and rely solely on accuracy. However, cross-model prior agreement falls below 80%, and over 15% of items can be answered correctly without any image, causing accuracy to overestimate prior resistance. To address these limitations, we introduce ViSiP-Bench, a benchmark grounded in rare-but-real scenarios across six prior categories, with a model-aware dynamic prior induction protocol and three metrics that more precisely and continuously measure prior resistance than accuracy alone. Evaluating 24 models reveals that prior adherence is pervasive, does not improve with scale, and larger models tend to either strongly override their priors or firmly adhere to them. We therefore propose MAPS, which identifies each model's strongest priors and fine-tunes on factually grounded knowledge to soften excessive prior reliance without disrupting general capabilities.</p></details> |  |
| **[TOPS: First-Principles Visual Token Pruning via Constructing Token Optimal Preservation Sets for Efficient MLLM Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.27161v3)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) have achieved strong multimodal reasoning capabilities, but their efficiency is limited by the large number of visual tokens, which introduces substantial computational overhead. Visual token pruning offers a natural solution, yet existing methods are imperfect: attention-based criteria tend to retain redundant tokens, while diversity-based criteria are often agnostic to user instructions. Even methods that combine multiple criteria still lack a principled formulation of the intrinsic objective of token pruning. In this paper, we revisit visual token pruning from a first-principles perspective and formulate it as constructing Token Optimal Preservation Sets. Through a top-down information-theoretic analysis, we identify three fundamental principles for effective token selection: Task Relevance, Information Coverage, and Semantic Diversity. Based on these principles, we propose TOPS, a training-free and model-agnostic pruning module that can be applied to various MLLMs. Extensive experiments on 7 MLLM backbones and 14 benchmarks demonstrate that TOPS outperforms prior methods under diverse pruning settings. Notably, on LLaVA-NeXT, TOPS removes 77.8% of visual tokens while preserving 100.0% and 100.6% performance on its 7B and 13B models, respectively, suggesting that pruning redundant visual tokens can sometimes mitigate hallucination and inspire future lightweight MLLM design. Our code is available at https://github.com/MAX-7237/TOPS.</p></details> | 27 pages, 18 figures |
| **[MiCo: Mutual Information Coverage Optimization through Semantic Erasure Modeling for Efficient MLLM Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.34330v3)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) have demonstrated impressive performance in multimodal understanding, but processing large numbers of visual tokens results in high computational costs. While many methods have been proposed to reduce the number of visual tokens, most of them rely on heuristics and are prone to discarding substantial visual information during pruning, leading to degradation in model performance. In this work, by using a semantic erasure model, we derive a general mutual information coverage objective from task log-loss and propose MiCo, a training-free two-stage pruning method. MiCo first uses visual signals to select a representative candidate pool before visual tokens enter the language model, then performs task-aware subset selection within it. At each stage, suitable observable proxies instantiate the derived objective as a monotone submodular coverage function, which MiCo greedily optimizes under the token budget. MiCo is evaluated on diverse MLLMs ranging from 7B to 13B parameters across a broad range of image and video benchmarks spanning general visual reasoning, fine-grained OCR and grounding, hallucination detection, and long-video understanding. MiCo consistently achieves the best performance across nearly all evaluated models under all pruning ratios. On LLaVA-NEXT-13B, MiCo uses only 5.6% visual tokens, retains 97.5% of baseline performance, and achieves a 3.8-fold inference speedup. Our experiments demonstrate the effectiveness of MiCo and our mutual information coverage objective for visual token pruning. Our code is available at https://github.com/MAX-7237/MiCo.</p></details> | <details><summary>48 pa...</summary><p>48 pages, 28 tables, 17 figures</p></details> |
| **[Filling Before Advancing: Capability-Gap-Driven Post-Training for Scenario-Specialized Remote Sensing MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22205v3)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Remote sensing multimodal large language models (RS-MLLMs) have advanced aerial-image understanding, but scenario specialization remains constrained by scarce high-quality supervision and incomplete capability coverage. | **[Beyond Static Cues: Unified Dynamic Detection of MLLM Backdoors via Dual-Flow Trajectory Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20319v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) are increasingly deployed in safety-critical domains, yet their susceptibility to backdoor attacks remains a formidable challenge. | **[VisionWeave: Weaving Elastic Visual Representations as a Native Capability of MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.07987v1)** | 2026-10-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal large language models have become the dominant paradigm for visual understanding, but incur substantial costs by encoding inputs into dense, fixed-size patch tokens. Downsampling sacrifices this detail, while existing token pruning and adaptive approaches remain limited in content-adaptive granularity, task generalization, and integration with modern MLLMs and serving infrastructure. Overcoming these limitations calls for foundation models that learn, end to end, where-and at what granularity-to allocate visual representations, a native capability we term elastic visual representation weaving. We introduce VisionWeave, establishing this capability in frontier-level MLLMs through large-scale training. It combines two components: a gated spatial pooler constructs coarse-grained representations alongside native fine-grained representations within a shared MRoPE coordinate, while a granularity router learns their content-adaptive allocation. Through self-distillation alone, we validate this capability on Qwen3.5-4B and scale to Qwen3.8-27B with over 30K A100 GPU-hours. Based on Qwen3.8-27B, VisionWeave adaptively adjusts token savings to visual content, saving 43.0% tokens on average while retaining 98.9% native performance across eight benchmarks, versus only 88% performance preserved for token pruning baselines with a fixed 50% savings target. When deployed on SGLang serving engine, our method achieves a 2.3x throughput gain while reducing mean TTFT by 54.4% and mean TPOT by 60.6%. | **[CVIF: A Criticality-Driven Visual Intervention Framework for Geometric Diagram Understanding in MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.06399v1)** | 2026-10-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Despite significant progress in visual tasks by Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs), geometric diagram understanding remains challenging due to the presence of sparse visual cues and ambiguous symbol-primitive associations. Our results quantitatively demonstrate performance disparities across models and tasks, and highlight a substantial gap between current MLLM capabilities and human expert performance in front-end engineering. The FullFront benchmark and code are available in https://github.com/Mikivishy/FullFront.</p></details> |  |
| **[Human-Like Attention? This study investigates whether Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) exhibit human-like difficulty signatures in visual search tasks. However, differences were found as well: whereas humans invested extra search time to respond accurately on target-absent trials, MLLMs exhibited extreme present/absent response biases in complex searches. We conclude that MLLMs replicate high-level human performance signatures, yet their underlying computations differ significantly.</p></details> |  |
| **[Confidence under Visual Token Pruning: Removed Evidence and Risk-Controlled Token Budgets for MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.12035v4)** | 2026-10-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Visual token pruning speeds up multimodal large language models (MLLMs) by keeping a small subset of the visual tokens, and pruning methods are compared by the accuracy they retain. Pruning errors concentrate on questions whose evidence the selector removed, and confidence does not register the removal. When the queried object loses all of its tokens, accuracy on these questions drops from 59% to 17%, while confidence stays at the unpruned level. Returning a few object tokens to the kept set recovers most of the lost accuracy. Selectors that keep the most attended tokens remove such evidence most often and produce confident errors, which temperature scaling cannot re-rank. Selectors that avoid keeping redundant tokens stay close to the calibration of the unpruned model. We then use the confidence of the pruned model to set a per-question token budget. The model answers with few tokens first and again with all tokens when its confidence is low. Conformal risk control sets the threshold to bound the expected deviation from the unpruned model. With coverage-based selection, this cascade needs about a third of the prefill tokens of the unpruned model, while with FastV it needs more than the unpruned model. The savings come mainly from how well the confidence ranks the answers that differ from the unpruned ones.</p></details> |  |

| **[PFAdapter: Hierarchical LoRA Decomposition for Personalized Federated MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12111v2)** | 2026-10-03 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Agentic AI systems are reshaping communications and networking by deploying autonomous intelligent agents capable of collaborative learning while maintaining data privacy at network edges. To address these challenges, we propose PFAdapter, a communication-efficient framework introducing hierarchical LoRA decomposition to explicitly separate adapter parameters into global-shared and local-private components. Query and key projections are assigned to global synchronization for capturing universal multimodal semantics across the network, while value and output projections remain localized for edge-specific adaptation. Additionally, orthogonality regularization based on the Frobenius norm enforces strict separation between these components, preventing redundant feature learning. Selective aggregation protocols synchronize only global-shared components across the federated network, preserving local expertise and reducing communication costs by nearly 50%. Extensive experiments on VQA-RAD, SLAKE, Hateful Memes, and CrisisMMD datasets demonstrate that PFAdapter consistently outperforms state-of-the-art baselines, achieving accuracy improvements ranging from 2.4% to 4.8% across diverse edge intelligence tasks. | **[Continual Learning with Elastic Regularization and Synthetic Replay for Federated MLLM Fine-Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12112v2)** | 2026-10-03 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Federated fine-tuning of Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) across distributed networks enables privacy-sensitive adaptation to evolving data streams, yet a fundamental obstacle prevents robust deployment in dynamic environments: catastrophic forgetting, wherein sequential task updates erase previously acquired knowledge across visual, linguistic, and cross-modal representations. At the aggregation level, Task-similarity-aware gradient aggregation autonomously filters and reweights client updates by gradient cosine similarity, suppressing conflicting directions and stabilizing the global learning trajectory. | **[Why MLLMs Struggle to Count: Overcoming Individuation and Aggregation Bottlenecks with ConvStack](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.37096v2)** | 2026-10-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) consistently struggle with fine-grained visual counting, yet the underlying causes remain poorly understood. | **[TerraVis: Towards Evaluation of World-Grounded Visual Consistency in Text-to-Image Generation via MLLM Workflows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02959v1)** | 2026-10-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent text-to-image models have made substantial progress in photorealism, aesthetics, and text-image alignment. | **[Video-MemComp: Taylor-Guided Sensitivity-Aware Memory Management for Streaming Video Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32784v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The rapid growth of video data has created an urgent need for efficient video understanding systems that can handle long and continuous visual inputs, posing significant challenges for Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) due to severe memory bottlenecks. Moreover, existing compression methods often overlook the explosive accumulation of approximation errors. To address this issue, we propose Video-MemComp, a training-free and sensitivity-aware context management framework for memory-constrained streaming video understanding. Our method theoretically decomposes compression error into orthogonal local approximation error and layer-wise structural sensitivity via a first-order Taylor expansion. To minimize local approximation error, we perform token aggregation in a pre-RoPE safe zone with an attention-conserving logit bias, mitigating geometric distortions caused by RoPE rotations. To manage layer-wise structural sensitivity, we dynamically allocate the KV cache budget based on offline gradient priors and online context entropy, prioritizing high-sensitivity layers. Video-MemComp outperforms or achieves performance comparable to full-cache and state-of-the-art compression methods on benchmarks including MLVU, VideoMME, and EgoSchema. Notably, it improves the overall accuracy of Qwen2.5-VL-3B from 62.3% to 64.1% while reducing the KV cache to an equivalent of 6,000 tokens per layer, saving over 90% of KV memory.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted by ACM MM 2026. Project Page: https://hhnk666.github.io/Video-MemComp.github.io</p></details> |

(ii) A persistent binding gap: even when P and R are both correct on the same stem, models still answer F incorrectly 51.2% of the time. (iii) The bottleneck is S3: process diagnostics and Stage-wise Stimulus Augmentation localize the dominant failure to rule-to-instance mapping. (iv) Scaling and thinking do not help: neither larger models nor explicit thinking mode reliably closes the gap, and thinking even lowers rule and full-item accuracy. StemBind reframes AVR evaluation from final-answer ranking to locating where abstract visual reasoning breaks down, identifying rule-to-instance binding as a concrete next target for vision-grounded reasoning.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at NeurIPS 2026 (Evaluations and Datasets Track). Camera-ready version. Project page: https://hexixiang.github.io/StemBind ; Code: https://github.com/hexixiang/StemBind ; Data: https://huggingface.co/datasets/user48271/Stembind</p></details> |
| **[Making MLLM Latents Matter: Factor-Aware Predictive Learning for Cinematic Video Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30926v2)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Cinematic video generation requires both temporal inference from limited inputs (i.e., sparse rather than frame-by-frame guidance) and factor-aware control of character, scene, action, and camera. First, latent-only generator grounding trains the DiT with a frozen MLLM and connector, strengthening the DiT's use of MLLM latents for generation. Second, for factor-aware control, paired videos specify a target-factor change and non-target preservation, and factor-span replacement trains the DiT to realize this relationship in its output. Third, for temporal inference, text-only and text-plus-complete-video observations share a video target, and the trained DiT is then frozen while the MLLM and connector are optimized through it. This final stage trains limited-input representations for generation while retaining complete-observation reconstruction as a complementary training mode. Extensive experiments demonstrate our consistently better performance on three cinematic video generation tasks (i.e., sparse-to-video, factor-aware editing, and multi-shot generation) over baselines and validate FAPL's contributions to MLLM feature use, temporal control, and factor coordination.</p></details> |  |

| **[Beyond Post-Hoc Rewards: Privileged Reasoning Exploration for Multimodal Embeddings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32474v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Advances in Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have driven substantial progress in multimodal embeddings. | **[Asking a Multimodal LLM Whether Someone Fell: Zero-Shot Screening of Low-Resolution Thermal Video in a Laboratory and an Occupied Home](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29913v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Thermal cameras can watch over older people living alone without capturing faces, and multimodal large language models (LLMs) can judge a short video without task-specific training. We evaluate a production multimodal LLM, Gemini 3.8 Flash at temperature 0, as the decision stage of an event-triggered fall screener, used exactly as a deployed prototype uses it: one request per short thermal clip, with its sound track when there is one, and its predecessor Gemini 3.7 Flash on the same requests. On 438 staged falls and 563 non-fall windows that we located by hand in a public 32x24 thermal dataset, the deployed request called 69% of the held-out subjects' falls (67% over all subjects; bed falls excluded) and none of 499 non-fall windows, including people lying on the floor and getting up. Over 76 h of ordinary life in an occupied room, recorded at 160x120 with sound, it called none of 395 clips a fall (95% upper bound 1.2 false alarms per day) and caught 4 of the 9 staged falls the camera could see. One cautionary sentence in the prompt ("a person who goes out of view ... is not a fall") accounted for many of the misses: removing it raised sensitivity on the held-out subjects from 69% to 83% on 3.8 Flash and from 80% to 92% on Gemini 3.7 Flash, and in the home from 4 to 9 of the 9 visible falls, with no false call in ordinary living but at the price of calling deliberate lie-downs a fall. Sensitivity collapsed at 8x6 pixels (34%); identical requests disagreed on 4% of windows; and when the model called a fall, its explanation placed it within a second of the annotated landing 99% of the time. Two conventional detectors trained on the laboratory data caught nearly every held-out laboratory fall but raised 14.2 and 23.9 false alarms per day in the home. We release the frame-level fall annotations of the public dataset and every model answer on it.</p></details> | <details><summary>13 pa...</summary><p>13 pages, 6 figures. Data: https://senecta.ai/research/thermal-fall-llm-data.zip</p></details> |
| **[AT-Differ: Dataset for Audio-Text Difference Captioning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29836v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Evaluating audio-text semantic alignment is important in various audio-language tasks. Existing methods mainly quantify audio-text semantic alignment using a single score and cannot explicitly describe how audio and text differ. In this paper, we propose audio-text difference captioning, a task that generates textual descriptions of differences between audio and text. We also construct a new dataset AT-DIFFER containing audio-text pairs with systematically simulated differences and corresponding difference captions. Using AT-DIFFER, we fine-tune an existing multimodal large language model to describe differences between audio and text. The experimental results show that the original model has limited ability to describe such differences, whereas fine-tuning on AT-DIFFER substantially improves this ability.</p></details> | <details><summary>Submi...</summary><p>Submitted to ICASSP 2027</p></details> |
| **[CoLVR: Enhancing Exploratory Latent Visual Reasoning via Contrastive Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.08802v4)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Due to the potential for exploratory reasoning of Latent Visual Reasoning, recent works tend to enable MLLMs (Multimodal Large Language Models) to perform visual reasoning by propagating continuous hidden states instead of decoding intermediate steps into discrete tokens. However, existing works typically rely on hard alignment objectives to force latent representations to match predefined visual features, thereby severely limiting the exploratory of latent reasoning process. To address this problem, we propose CoLVR (Contrastive Optimization for Latent Visual Reasoning). To obtain a more exploratory visual reasoning, CoLVR introduces a latent contrastive training framework. Firstly, CoLVR learns diverse and exploratory representations with a latent contrastive objective guided by angle-based perturbation, which expands the semantic latent space and avoids over-constrained embedding. Then, CoLVR employs a latent trajectory contrastive reward for RL (Reinforcement Learning) post-training to enable fine-grained optimization of latent visual reasoning process and thus fostering diverse reasoning behaviors. Experiments demonstrate that CoLVR significantly enhances the exploratory capability of latent representations, achieving average improvements of 5.83% on VSP and 8.00% on Jigsaw, while also outperforming existing latent models on out of domain benchmarks, with a 3.40% gain on MMStar. The data, codes, and models are released at https://github.com/Oscar-dzy/CoLVR.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted by NeurIPS 2026. 17 pages, 10 figures, 9 tables</p></details> |
| **[Towards Explainable Industrial Anomaly Detection via Knowledge-Guided Latent Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.09850v3)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Industrial anomaly detection demands precise reasoning over fine-grained defect patterns. However, existing multimodal large language models (MLLMs), pretrained on general-domain data, often struggle to capture category-specific anomalies, thereby limiting both detection accuracy and interpretability. To address these limitations, we propose Reason-IAD, a knowledge-guided dynamic latent reasoning framework for explainable industrial anomaly detection. Reason-IAD comprises two core components. First, a retrieval-augmented knowledge module incorporates category-specific textual descriptions into the model input, enabling context-aware reasoning over domain-specific defects. Second, an entropy-driven latent reasoning mechanism conducts iterative exploration within a compact latent space using optimizable latent think tokens, guided by an entropy-based reward that encourages confident and stable predictions. Furthermore, a dynamic visual injection strategy selectively incorporates the most informative image patches into the latent sequence, directing the reasoning process toward regions critical for anomaly detection. Extensive experimental results demonstrate that Reason-IAD consistently outperforms state-of-the-art methods across multiple tasks. The code will be publicly available at https://github.com/chenpeng052/Reason-IAD.</p></details> |  |
| **[CausGround: Causal Interaction Grounding for Adaptive Embodied Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29702v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) show promising reasoning capabilities, but reliable physical interaction requires planning that accounts for when actions are executable and how they change the world. These preconditions and effects are environment-dependent and often not captured by MLLMs, making adaptation challenging. Yet existing approaches either require costly environment-specific training or rely on unstructured interaction experience that is difficult to reuse. Therefore, we introduce CausGround, a non-parametric causal interaction grounding framework that distills interaction experience into reusable action-centered causal knowledge. CausGround identifies action preconditions and effects and organizes them in a factorized world-state representation capturing object attributes and inter-object relations. At planning time, task-relevant causal dependencies are grounded in current observations to support backward reasoning over preconditions and forward reasoning over effects for action selection and multi-step planning. We evaluate CausGround on simulated high-level planning, interaction reasoning, and real-world robot manipulation. On adapted VirtualHome and RoboCasa benchmarks, CausGround improves vision-based high-level planning across MLLM backbones, increasing average task success from 24.9% to 53.3% on VirtualHome and closed-loop planning success from 43.0% to 57.0% on RoboCasa. On SWITCH, average interaction reasoning accuracy increases from 25.8% to 31.9%, indicating improved action selection, recovery, and verification. Moreover, real-world experiments show that CausGround reasons backward from MLLM-interpreted goals to derive executable high-level action sequences for sequential manipulation. These results demonstrate that reusable action preconditions and effects support long-horizon embodied planning across diverse environments without parameter updates.</p></details> | <details><summary>Proje...</summary><p>Project Page: https://causground.github.io</p></details> |
| **[CountVideoBench: A Benchmark to Evaluate Prediction Biases on the Object Counting task by Video-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28356v2)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Object counting in videos remains a poorly characterized capability of multimodal large language models. Counting questions are included in many existing VideoQA benchmarks, but the ground-truth count distributions in these benchmarks are heavily skewed toward small values. Across the nine benchmarks we survey, the median ground-truth count is at most five, and the three most frequent values together account for between 45% and 93% of the videos. The resulting mean accuracy is then dominated by the most frequent counts; a value-dependent bias at a rare count contributes negligibly to the average and remains invisible. We introduce CountVideoBench, a benchmark of 2,600 synthetic videos covering 26 cardinalities in the interval [5, 50] (100 videos each), grouped into six clusters of consecutive counts that span the range while enabling near-neighbor comparisons. Each video contains a single class of static objects under a controlled camera motion, removing object motion and reducing recognition as confounds. We evaluate sixteen video-language models from nine architecture families in a zero-shot setting. No model exceeds 27.6% Exact Match. The dependence of accuracy on cardinality varies across models, ranging from monotonic decline to a non-monotonic peak-and-collapse pattern, which rules out increasing difficulty with cardinality as the dominant explanation of counting failure. Every model further concentrates its predictions on its own set of preferred integers, with accuracy peaks coinciding with these values and collapsing at adjacent counts. These findings highlight the need for balanced, diagnostic counting benchmarks to reliably assess this fundamental visual capability.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at BMVC 2026. Project page: https://countvideobench.github.io</p></details> |
| **[Generalized CycleGRPO for Fine-Grained Multimodal Understanding and Grounding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29552v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Fine-grained multimodal intelligence requires models to both understand a designated region and localize it from language. Existing methods learn these directions with separate objectives and task-specific annotations. We present Generalized CycleGRPO, the first unified reinforcement learning framework coupling region understanding and localization across spatial, temporal, and spatio-temporal domains. Given a region, a multimodal large language model generates candidate descriptions and then localizes each description to reconstruct the input region. Reconstruction overlap provides a verifiable reward for captioning, while individual localization rewards improve the inverse mapping. A common region-space formulation extends this cycle from image masks and boxes to video intervals and timestamped spatial evidence, without reference captions or an external language-model judge during reinforcement learning. In videos, the cycle encourages descriptions that distinguish a target event from similar recurrences. We introduce DOVE (Distinctive Occurrence-level Video-caption Evaluation), a human-verified contrastive benchmark for this temporal distinctiveness. CycleGRPO improves SAMTok's overall gIoU from 57.5% to 68.2% on GroundingSuite and average gIoU from 71.2% to 78.7% on GRES. For temporal tasks, it achieves 51.9% tIoU on Charades and 33.3% Temporal Evidence Coverage on DOVE, compared with 45.6% and 14.8% for the Qwen3-VL initialization. These results establish region-text cycle consistency as a general self-evaluation principle for multimodal understanding and localization. Models, code, and DOVE are available at https://devinxzhang.github.io/Generalized-CycleGRPO-Page/.</p></details> | <details><summary>31 pa...</summary><p>31 pages, 14 figures, 16 tables</p></details> |
| **[Balancing Foundation Models and Trait-Specific Learning for Forehead-Crease Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29495v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Biometric recognition often degrades under partial facial vis- ibility, where conventional facial cues are occluded. The forehead region remains consistently visible and contains distinctive crease patterns that can serve as a complementary biometric trait. We present a unified eval- uation of forehead-crease recognition across explainable zero-shot rea- soning, domain-adaptive learning, and trait-specific modeling. FIRE en- ables interpretable zero-shot verification using multimodal large language models, while pretrained face-recognition models are adapted to the fore- head domain through margin-based learning. Under open-set evaluation, the best zero-shot model GPT-4o+FIRE achieves 18.22% EER, while Edge-Forehead achieves 8.30% EER and 54.01% TMR@0.1% FMR. The results show that although MLLMs offer interpretable zero-shot verifi- cation, trait-specific learning remains more effective for robust forehead- crease biometric recognition.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at CVIP 2026</p></details> |
| **[LLMSeg: Training-Free Text-Prompted Image Segmentation with B-Splines](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29409v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Promptable segmentation models such as SAM 3 achieve remarkable open-vocabulary performance but require dedicated architectures, large-scale annotation engines, and task-specific training. We ask a different question: can a general-purpose multimodal large language model (MLLM) segment images directly, without an added segmentation head, mask decoder, or training? We present LLMSeg, a training-free pipeline that turns a vision-capable LLM into a text-prompted image segmenter. LLMSeg decomposes segmentation into three LLM-driven stages. First, detect localizes all instances matching a free-form text query and describes their coarse morphology. Next, segment traces each instance using closed periodic B-splines confined to its detected box. These splines define an outer boundary and optional hole loops. Finally, refine iteratively proposes focus boxes around residual errors and retraces confined splines. The process ends when the mask stabilizes or the pass budget is exhausted. The output is a binary mask with inspectable intermediate geometry. We evaluate LLMSeg across ten referring and reasoning segmentation splits (ReasonSeg and RefCOCO/+/g). We also evaluate open-vocabulary instance segmentation (LVIS, COCO, and SA-Co), semantic segmentation, and zero-shot detection (ODinW13 and RF-100VL). With an off-the-shelf Gemini-3.7-Flash backend, LLMSeg is competitive with and often surpasses the specialist SAM 3 model on identical items. It reaches 71.5 cIoU on ReasonSeg val without additional segmentation-specific training. Backend model updates can improve accuracy without retraining the pipeline. The code is available at https://abdullahamdi.com/llmseg .</p></details> | preprint |
| **[LocalAgent: Collaborative Agentic Verification for Fine-Grained Instance-Level Consistency](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29174v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) have made rapid progress in general visual understanding, yet they still struggle with fine-grained instance-level consistency verification, where the goal is to determine whether two images depict the exact same physical object under varying viewpoints, illumination, and backgrounds. This task requires both global semantic perception and precise examination of subtle local evidence, since semantically similar objects may differ only in small suspicious regions. To address this challenge, we propose LocalAgent, a collaborative agentic framework that integrates the complementary strengths of a generalist MLLM and lightweight specialist verifiers. The MLLM first reasons globally to identify suspicious local regions, which are then delegated to a Local Evidence Verifier (LEV) toolkit for fine-grained appearance and geometric matching. LEV comprises Local Appearance Consistency (LAC), which estimates appearance similarity under illumination and color changes, and Local Geometric Correspondence (LGC), which estimates spatial correspondence under viewpoint changes. LEV returns structured scalar evidence that the MLLM integrates for the final decision. We further introduce an auxiliary-capped reinforcement fine-tuning (RFT) strategy, in which answer correctness dominates auxiliary tool-use and format signals, enabling the policy to learn when verification is necessary. Experiments show that LocalAgent improves mean accuracy by 4.9% over the strongest CoT-RFT baseline while invoking a verifier on only 12.13% of samples and preserving the general multimodal capabilities of the underlying MLLM. The open-source repository is available at https://github.com/Firefly-zoo/LocalAgent-main.</p></details> | 22 pages, 4 figures |
| **[Human Cognitive Benchmarks Reveal Foundational Visual Gaps in MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.16435v5)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Humans develop perception through a bottom-up hierarchy: from basic primitives and Gestalt principles to high-level semantics. Current Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) are trained directly on complex downstream tasks. Does it guarantee their performance on these foundational visual capabilities? To systematically investigate this gap, we introduce VisFacor, a benchmark that digitizes 20 vision-centric subtests from FRCT, a well-established cognitive psychology assessment spanning four domains of human visual cognition. Furthermore, we design algorithms to automatically construct and validate unlimited test cases with controllable difficulty. Using VisFacor, we evaluate 39 frontier MLLMs, including both proprietary (e.g., GPT, Gemini) and open-source (e.g., LLaMA, Qwen) models. The best model achieves a score of only 55.9%. Analysis reveals good internal consistency (Cronbach's alpha = 0.94) and construct validity (compared to existing vision benchmarks). Models consistently fail on tasks such as mental rotation, spatial relation inference, and figure-ground discrimination, regardless of model size or prompting strategy. These findings suggest that performance improvements on existing general benchmarks might represent castles in the air instead of a genuine mastery of human-like visual cognition.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to NeurIPS 2026</p></details> |
| **[MARS: Harmonizing Multimodal Convergence via Adaptive Rank Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.00720v3)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Fine-tuning Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) with parameter-efficient methods like Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) is crucial for task adaptation. However, imbalanced training dynamics across modalities often lead to suboptimal accuracy due to negative interference, a challenge typically addressed with inefficient heuristic methods such as manually tuning separate learning rates. To overcome this, we introduce MARS (Multimodal Adaptive Rank Search), an approach to discover optimal rank pairs that balance training dynamics while maximizing performance. Our key innovation, a proposed framework of dual scaling laws, enables this search: one law models module-specific convergence time to prune the search space to candidates with aligned dynamics, while the other predicts final task performance to select the optimal pair from the pruned set. By re-purposing the LoRA rank as a controller for modality-specific convergence speed, MARS outperforms baseline methods and provides a robust, automated strategy for optimizing MLLM fine-tuning.</p></details> | <details><summary>NeurI...</summary><p>NeurIPS 2026; Project Page: this https URL: https://minkyoungcho.github.io/mars/</p></details> |
| **[Small Average Error, Wide Individual Error: General-Purpose LLMs Estimating Energy Intake from Meal Photos in a Feeding Trial](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26538v2)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Photo-based estimation of food energy could ease dietary assessment, and general-purpose multimodal large language models (LLMs) make such estimates easy to obtain. Most evaluations use one photo taken before eating and compare with energy served, while dietary assessment needs energy eaten. We evaluated eight models from three vendors (Claude Haiku, Sonnet and Opus 5.5; GPT-6 luna, 6.1 sol and 6 astra; Gemini 3.8 Flash and 4 Argon) on 802 meals from ACETADA, a controlled feeding trial with weighed intake, with the before photo only, before and after photos in separate calls (subtraction), or both photos in one request. Three results ran against intuition. Subtraction under-estimated intake for six of eight models, because the models still report hundreds of kilocalories for a nearly empty tray. Scaling the before-only estimate by the average share eaten (0.87) matched much of the gain from the after photo; beyond that constant, the second photo lowered meal-level error by only 2.2 to 4.4 points, mainly in meals with food left. With one photo, two models had a near-zero intake bias only because they under-estimated the served energy and ignored uneaten food. In ablations, adding the after photo improved accuracy under both prompt formats for seven models, while prompt effects varied across models. Over 148 feeding days, five models kept the 90% confidence interval of their mean error within a pre-set 10% margin; one model also met the pre-set 30% limits of agreement, narrowly (-28% to +27%; limits outside 30% cannot be excluded), and the other seven did not. Protein was under-estimated by every model, most in protein-dense meals; repeated calls changed error by at most 1.4 points; a high reasoning effort helped only the smallest model. A low average bias is not evidence of accurate individual intake estimation; such estimates should support, not replace, assessment by a dietitian.</p></details> | <details><summary>20 pa...</summary><p>20 pages, 1 figure, 12 tables. v2: adds Gemini 3.8 Flash and Gemini 4 Argon under a pre-registered addendum; all tables recomputed over eight models; conclusions unchanged except that one model now meets the pre-set limits of agreement, narrowly. | **[InterWebCoder: Learning Interactive Webpage Generation from Executable Tests via Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28610v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Interactive webpages underpin modern applications, but reproducing both their visual appearance and multi-step behavior remains challenging for multimodal large language models (MLLMs). Scalable training data must capture user actions, state transitions, and expected outcomes, while real-world webpage code is often entangled with irrelevant scripts and external dependencies. Moreover, existing GUI-agent-based verification provides slow and potentially unstable functional feedback. To address these challenges, we propose InterWebCoder, an efficient framework for interactive webpage code generation. InterWebCoder introduces a Test-Specification-Guided and Execution-Verified Data Synthesis pipeline that records real-world interaction trajectories and synthesizes self-contained webpage code with executable test scripts. A failureattributed repair mechanism uses runtime evidence to locate and iteratively fix errors in either the webpage code or test script. We then combine test-ID-weighted supervised fine-tuning with group-relative policy optimization, directly optimizing functional correctness and visual fidelity using test pass rates and state-level visual similarity. This replaces costly GUI-agent verification with fast, reproducible executable feedback. Our pipeline produces 1,736 verified samples from 2,058 tasks, achieving an 84.35% synthesis success rate. On IWR-Bench and Vision2Web, InterWebCoder achieves final scores of 48.54 and 48.42 with a 9B backbone, outperforming 30B-level models and Gemini-3.7-Flash while remaining competitive with Claude-Sonnet-5. It reduces reward-computation latency by over 97%, eliminates external MLLM verification costs, and accelerates end-to-end training by up to 1.4x.</p></details> |  |
| **[Code2Video-Compass: A Comprehensive Benchmark for General-Purpose Code-Based Video Generation across T2V, I2V, and V2V](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28416v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Code-based video generation offers fine-grained control, targeted editing, and component reuse compared with direct video generation. This approach requires multimodal large language models (MLLMs) to translate visual and temporal requirements into executable programs that produce complete videos. However, existing code-based benchmarks primarily target specialized tasks, leaving complete video creation across diverse creative categories and input modalities insufficiently evaluated. We introduce Code2Video-Compass, a general-purpose benchmark for code-based video generation across text-to-video (T2V), image-to-video (I2V), and video-to-video (V2V) settings. Code2Video-Compass comprises 2,104 tasks spanning 10 categories, 24 subtypes, and three complexity dimensions covering construction, dynamic animation, and state dependency. It combines tasks curated from textual instructions and visual references with corresponding T2V, I2V, and V2V tasks derived from complete professional and user-created videos. We propose a dual-level evaluation framework combining 12 objective metrics and five subjective metrics. Objective evaluation covers operational performance, video quality, text-video alignment, and reference fidelity, while subjective evaluation uses an MLLM judge to evaluate instruction following and holistic video quality. Extensive evaluation of 15 recent proprietary and open-weight MLLMs from nine families reveals that even leading models face challenges in reliably generating high-quality complete videos aligned with textual and visual requirements, with performance generally declining as construction, dynamic animation, and state-dependency complexity increases. We anticipate that Code2Video-Compass will advance reliable, coherent, and controllable code-based video generation and inspire broader progress in programmatic visual creation.</p></details> |  |
| **[DIVE: Benchmarking Full-Duplex Interaction with Visual Engagement in Continuous Streams](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28405v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent advances in multimodal large language models (MLLMs) have substantially improved spoken interaction and video understanding. Existing online and streaming benchmarks primarily assess causal multimodal perception through timestamped question answering, whereas real-world interaction requires models to continuously identify task-relevant multimodal cues and decide whether, when, and how to respond. This leaves open how well current models can sustain real-time interaction throughout an evolving multimodal stream. To address this gap, we introduce DIVE, a benchmark for evaluating full-duplex interaction with visual engagement under continuous and realistic multimodal streams. It organizes six tasks into three complementary interaction scenarios. (1) Proactive Intervention evaluates whether models can identify emerging visual or semantic cues and intervene at the appropriate moment. (2) Continuous Engagement evaluates sustained participation through continuous visual tracking and visually grounded query interaction. (3) Interaction Coordination evaluates how models coordinate listening and speaking under overlapping and multi-party interactions. DIVE contains 525 audio/video samples with fine-grained human annotations and precise temporal metadata. Its evaluation protocol separates response quality, participation decisions, and response timing. We evaluate representative real-time models using response quality, window response rates, and reaction latency. Experiments show that leading real-time models achieve sub-second average response latency, yet their average window response rates remain at or below 53.77%. Response quality varies substantially across models and tasks, with adapting ongoing responses to newly arriving information remaining particularly challenging. We hope DIVE facilitates progress toward more natural and capable real-time multimodal interactive systems.</p></details> | <details><summary>Prepr...</summary><p>Preprint. 19 pages, 4 figures</p></details> |
| **[ChartEvo: Self-Evolving Agent Harnesses for Long-Tail Chart Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28282v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) have substantially advanced chart understanding and reasoning, yet their performance remains highly uneven across chart types. While modern MLLMs perform well on common charts, their accuracy drops sharply on long-tail charts with diverse visual structures and reasoning requirements. We introduce \textbf{ChartEvo}, a recursive self-improvement framework that enables chart agents to adapt to underperforming chart types through experience-driven harness-level self-improvement. ChartEvo jointly evolves reusable chart-specific Skills and executable Tools, while learning reusable local tool-call chains to compose evolved capabilities and control the inference overhead of tool-augmented reasoning. On long-tail charts from ChartBench and ChartX, ChartEvo improves accuracy over its base MLLM, Qwen3.7-Plus, by \textbf{45.6\%} and \textbf{32.5\%}, respectively, demonstrating the effectiveness of recursive self-improvement for long-tail chart adaptation.</p></details> |  |
| **[VGL-stGen: Learning a shared multimodal model for lung histology and spatial transcriptomics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28266v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>VGL-stGen is a multimodal large language model adapted from a pretrained medical vision-language model using paired lung H&E and spatial transcriptomics with single-cell references. Training integrates image-expression and expression-cell-type pairs, and gene-conditioned pseudo-labels transfer transcriptomic cell-type supervision to histology images.
