Recovery does not confer usability. Entities like offer, eligibility, and activation recur across services under a shared vocabulary, related in name but operationally distinct, so one end-to-end journey implicates over a thousand near-identical rules that must not be conflated. Flat vector retrieval fails, returning an identical rule from the wrong service at high similarity with no signal of error. Content-level ontology, the traditional remedy, did not exist, and relating every occurrence of recurring entities would have cost more than the engagement and not survived a release cycle. We place the ontology at the artifact level. In this machine-generated corpus, each artifact retains its producer, component, service, domain and type, provenance that disambiguates before content is read. We present a knowledge layer that organizes before retrieving: a self-describing catalog, a sparse metadata registry, and a navigation hypergraph defined over artifacts rather than extracted entities. Queries resolve to artifact identifiers that constrain vector search, so the competing rule from another service is absent rather than outranked, making disambiguation structural, not statistical. Built in under a week and admitting new services by convention, the layer serves SDLC agents, developers, quality engineers and analysts. It supported root-cause analysis and resolution of 100+ production defects with developer verification and attributed 35 release regression failures to specific rules and services.</p></details> |  |

To operationalize this frontier, Frontier-OPD organizes on-policy experience into a shared-state trajectory graph, where trajectory-balanced aggregation of teacher-student disagreement identifies distinct intervention candidates while preserving the histories needed for replay. Short teacher bridges are then assessed by comparing agreement over the bridge and the ensuing student continuation with an equally long segment of the original trajectory, providing a behavioral proxy for guidance that the current student can build on. | **[AFRA: Age-Threshold Frame-Based Random Access for Timely Communication in Multi-Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32785v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Timely state updates are essential for coordination and monitoring in multi-agent systems, such as teams of mobile robots exchanging state information. Once its age reaches a threshold, an agent starts a local frame with a mandatory transmission and, if unsuccessful, makes one further attempt at a uniformly selected remaining slot. Each attempt carries a freshly generated update. AFRA requires only local state and acknowledgment feedback, with shared slot boundaries but independently timed local frames. We prove almost-sure convergence from any legal initial state to collision-free periodic operation when the frame length $L$ and age threshold $T$ satisfy $L\ge T>N\ge2$, where $N$ is the number of agents. | **[Every Edge Needs a Quote: Building a Quote-Grounded Knowledge Graph of Healthcare Software Adoption with Language-Model Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32781v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Hospitals regularly buy software that clinicians and patients depend on. We present a pipeline that builds a knowledge graph of customer and integration relations from the open web under one rule: every edge must be supported by a verbatim quote from its source page. A permissive extractor proposes relations with quotes, and a layered grounding stage decides which to keep, using a referent step that reads the whole page when a quote names only one side, deterministic checks, and a panel of five model calls voting on nine criteria. | **[A-MapReduce: Executing Wide Search via Agentic MapReduce](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.01331v2)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Contemporary large language model (LLM)-based multi-agent systems exhibit systematic advantages in deep research tasks, which emphasize iterative, vertically structured information seeking. | **[AM-Bench: A Unified Taxonomy and Evaluation Suite for Agentic Misalignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24264v3)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>As LLM-based agents are deployed with increasing autonomy in real-world environments, a critical failure mode emerges: agentic misalignment, where agents choose harmful actions over failure. However, existing safety benchmarks primarily evaluate whether agents comply with explicitly harmful instructions, leaving the conditions that cause agents to choose harmful actions poorly understood. To address this limitation, we propose AM-Bench, a unified taxonomy and 35 scenario evaluation benchmark spanning seven industries for agentic misalignment grounded in the fraud triangle, a well-established criminological framework explaining goal-directed misconduct. We adapt this framework to agentic environments and construct our evaluation suite and detection systems along three dimensions: perceived threat, perceived opportunity, and reasoning. We operationalize each dimension into structured subcategories rooted in prior theory: five threat categories derived from the instrumental convergence thesis, seven opportunity categories drawn from the Emergent Strategic Reasoning Risks (ESRR) taxonomy, and a novel four-question binary reasoning framework synthesized from prior literature. Our evaluation of 10 frontier models reveals a 62-percentage-point gap in misalignment across scenarios, with Gemini 3 Flash and Gemini 3.1 Pro exhibiting the highest levels of agentic misalignment while Claude Sonnet 4.6 displays near-zero rates across all conditions. Our reasoning framework further reveals that models may exhibit harmful behaviours without explicit situational awareness or explanation, complicating both mitigation and detection. AM-Bench scenarios and code are available at https://github.com/AI4Collaboration/Agentic-Misalignment-Bench</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted as a poster at NeurIPS 2026 Evaluations and Datasets Track</p></details> |
| **[Can AI Agents Help Robotic Engineers? Lessons from Two Focus Days in Industry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32636v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Software developers increasingly use AI agents that execute multi-step programming tasks on their own. However, it is unclear if and how this transfers to robotic engineering, where engineers program industrial robots in niche, vendor-specific languages and rely on visual feedback from simulation tools. Within this paper, we report a case study on the use of AI agents by an SME that programs industrial robots. Our results show that the agent did not make the engineers more productive, with the exception of a task that required the generation of highly repetitive code. However, the results with the agent improved over time, as the engineers learned how to use it and the agent was adapted to the domain. Moreover, the experience with AI mattered more for the success with the agent than the experience with robotic engineering. This shows that AI agents are not a plug-and-play solution. Instead, companies must train their engineers on the usage of agents, plan time for a learning curve, and ensure that agent configurations match the domain and developer requirements. The strong learning curve we observed further implies that studies on productivity gains with AI should be longitudinal, as they otherwise miss how engineers adopt agents in practice.</p></details> |  |
| **[Probe Before You Edit: Probing-Guided Molecular Optimization for LLM Agents in Structure-Based Drug Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.00555v3)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Structure-based drug design increasingly employs LLM agents to iteratively refine ligands against a target pocket, yet a viable ligand must satisfy two often-conflicting objectives---binding affinity and druggability---which single optimization steps rarely improve together. To quantify this difficulty, we introduce two diagnostic metrics: the first measures how often a single edit improves both objectives, and the second measures how often a gain on one objective comes with a loss on the other. Applying these diagnostics to current LLM-agent pipelines exposes a consistent failure mode: the agent performs molecular editing without knowing how the pocket-ligand complex responds to local modifications, thus rarely achieving joint improvement. Inspired by medicinal chemists, who probe the pocket-ligand complex with controlled analog edits before choosing an optimization direction, we propose \textbf{PROBE}, an optimization framework built around edit--response probing. PROBE first decomposes the ligand into editable sites and builds a pocket-specific \textbf{site map} that flags where joint gains are plausible, where the two objectives are likely in tension, and where liability substructures should be changed; it then performs controlled probe edits whose responses are distilled into an \textbf{EditManual}. Guided by the site map and EditManual, PROBE runs an iterative multi-agent optimization loop. On the CrossDocked2020 benchmark, PROBE achieves state-of-the-art performance and substantially mitigates the failure modes exposed by our diagnostics metrics. Codes are in https://github.com/yzf-code/PROBE/tree/main</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at NeurIPS 2026. Revised manuscript</p></details> |
| **[What Transfers Across Protein Pockets? Experience Reuse in Molecular Optimization for Agentic Structure-Based Drug Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32620v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM agents optimize a ligand in a protein pocket by editing it step by step and docking each edit. Recent agents first spend extra dockings probing the complex with controlled edits; guidance induced from these probes is reported to help more than the chemical knowledge the model already carries. The probes are then discarded with the instance that produced them, so the obvious next step is to keep them in an experience base, letting a new pocket inherit what earlier ones already paid for. Such a base is read through a \emph{key}: the record properties under which past edits are pooled and a new one is looked up. Using the full log of one such run over the CrossDocked2020 test-set pockets, we ask whether its keys recur on a pocket the base has never seen and still separate improving from worsening edits. \crchange{Using} the contacts at the site being edited, the action, and the round as \crchange{a} key, the base separates them sharply. Within our analysis, this separation is largely explained by how much the edit changed the molecule's size, the action, and the round, none of which describes the target. That is not because the target is irrelevant: naming it outright separates them several times more sharply. For a pocket absent from the base, transfer must rely on a description rather than its identity. Among the exact-match descriptions we evaluate, the more specific ones rarely recur, whereas the coarser ones provide little separation; the signals that do transfer primarily describe the edit and the molecule it produced rather than the target. Under the representations and lookup scheme studied here, an experience base may be more useful for prioritizing an agent's own proposals for docking than for directly prescribing what to build.</p></details> |  |
| **[Lessons Outlive Their Failures: A Case Study of Rules for AI Coding Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32578v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>AI coding agents increasingly work inside a harness: rule files such as AGENTS.md, on-demand skills, runtime hooks and automated checks. Most studies of harnesses look at a single point in time or at short benchmark runs. I report a six-week case study of my personal system of record, a plain-markdown repository that three AI agent tools edited daily under one shared rule set. I built a ledger of 118 agent failures, their fixes and whether they came back. I also tested the system's monthly LLM documentation audit against 47 known stale statements and 20 planted errors. Finally, I replayed five past failures in isolated git clones, with and without their fixes. All results come from one repository with three runs per condition, so I report them as exploratory. Stale documentation (drift) was the largest failure class (48 of 118), although 35 of those were found on one audit day. A single audit run found about half of the known drift (Claude Sonnet 5.5 mean: 50%), and at least 93% of its findings were correct. On Sonnet 5.5, the agent finished four of the five replayed tasks. In three of those four, the failure did not happen, with or without its fix. Only the stale-count failure came back without its check, and the check stopped it in every run. On older models, an agent drafted an em-dash once while the em-dash lint check was active, and the check rejected it. A written rule on run-time estimates did not change Sonnet 4.5's overestimates. These results suggest that harness lessons can outlive the failures they were written to fix, while drift persists and still needs mechanical checks. Tools that evolve a harness add lessons faster than they retire them, so they should re-test their rules from time to time and retire the obsolete ones.</p></details> | <details><summary>16 pa...</summary><p>16 pages, 4 figures, 4 tables</p></details> |
| **[Tool-Calling LLM Agents Under Realistic MCP Faults: Scale Does Not Purchase Fault Tolerance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32555v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM agents increasingly rely on external tools exposed via the Model Context Protocol (MCP), yet current benchmarks assume tools respond correctly and on time, leaving agent resilience to realistic tool failures untested. We introduce MCP-Injector, a drop-in, protocol-aware fault-injection middleware that interposes on the MCP tool-call path with controlled persistence (permanent, transient, intermittent) and seedable schedules. Its fault model is calibrated to the observed incident distribution (failure mode x persistence) of the public MCP ecosystem, mined from 57,240 issue and pull-request threads across 1,774 open-source MCP repositories. In a 1,350-cell experiment crossing two backbone sizes (20B, 120B), three agent stacks (custom multi-round plan-and-execute, OpenAI Agents SDK, LangChain), and three fault injection conditions, we find that faults drawn from this permanent-heavy stress profile cause severe quality degradation (task-completion score falls by 60%, from 8.08 to 3.23 out of 10), and that scale does not purchase fault tolerance for the two gpt-oss backbones we test: the 120B retains no larger share of its fault-free score under faults (resilience-ratio model, $p = 0.96$), and its completion rate under faults is, if anything, lower (91.1% vs. 95.6%; difference 4.4 points, 95% CI $[-0.2, 9.3]$). These results demonstrate that capability benchmarks alone can mask operational brittleness, motivating protocol-aware fault injection as a complementary evaluation dimension for tool-calling agents.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at NeurIPS 2026 (poster). 10 pages of main text plus appendices and the NeurIPS checklist. Code and data: https://gitlab.com/uniluxembourg/snt/sedan/papers/mcp-injector (archived at https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.23573765)</p></details> |
| **[On Task, Off Track: Benchmarking LLM and Agent Robustness to Near-Target Prompt Injection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32531v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Prompt injection benchmarks assess whether large language models (LLMs) and agents resist malicious instructions embedded in external content. However, a system could perform well by rejecting attacks that obviously abandon the user's request, while still accepting changes that retain most of a legitimate answer but make it incorrect or harmful. Testing more instructions for unrelated outcomes would not necessarily reveal this weakness. We call attacks seeking responses designed to resemble legitimate answers while introducing incorrect or harmful changes near-target prompt injection. We introduce NTBench, the Near-Target Benchmark, which builds on legitimate tasks from existing datasets. For each task, a legitimate reference outcome guides the generation of target--instruction pairs, with separate tracks targeting incorrect and harmful outcomes in responses or agent tool invocations. Harmful targets can also accompany otherwise correct task completion. We combine LLM-based checks with human validation of the intended deviations, then assess whether injected instructions induce them using response judgments or tool-call checks, excluding deviations already observed without injection. We evaluate seven LLMs on ten datasets for language tasks and two agent benchmarks. Under a common budget of three attack executions per sample, both tracks achieve higher overall attack success rates than the fixed-target baseline for every evaluated model in each setting. On language tasks, the increases range from 9.3 to 35.8 percentage points for incorrect targets and 1.7 to 17.3 percentage points for harmful targets. | **[A Guideline-Augmented Multi-Agent Framework for Schema-as-Code Biomedical Named Entity Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02970v3)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) have shown promising potential for biomedical named entity recognition (BioNER) through instruction following and in-context learning. Second, free-form generation lacks sufficient structural control, often leading to invalid formats, hallucinated mentions, duplicated entities, and boundary errors. To address these limitations, we propose GAMA, a guideline-augmented multi-agent framework for schema-as-code BioNER. GAMA first induces candidate annotation rules from labeled training instances and verifies them against annotated data to construct reliable dataset-specific guideline memory. Guided by these verified rules, a planning component generates ranked span-type hypotheses with rationales, and a coding component converts them into schema-constrained entity objects. A verification module then checks span grounding, type validity, and structural compliance, and performs dual-loop refinement to correct invalid or low-confidence predictions. Experiments on three widely used BioNER datasets using two different LLMs demonstrate the effectiveness of GAMA, achieving the best performance in four out of six LLM-dataset combinations. | **[When Agents Fail: Model or Harness--Who's to Blame?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32483v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM agents combine models with execution harnesses, so agentic benchmark performance reflects their joint behavior. We argue that harnesses should therefore be evaluated through their interactions with underlying models. We evaluate four general-purpose harnesses (OpenCode, Codex CLI, Pi CLI, and DeepAgents) with four core models, plus two fixed native model-harness systems, on six benchmarks covering context management, tool use, long-context planning, multimodal interaction, and artifact handoff. Including native references and stress conditions, 111 evaluation settings yield 52,168 trajectories. Harness rankings vary across models and benchmarks; task performance and valid completion can favor different pairings, and similar task scores can accompany distinct execution profiles. OpenCode ranks first in cross-model performance, while DeepSeek V4 Flash Preview maintains consistently high Mean VCR across harnesses on the shared four-benchmark panel. | **[WaitLens: Temporal VLM-Based Adaptive Waiting for Industrial GUI Testing Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32482v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Improving the efficiency of GUI testing agents is important for industrial deployment. To this end, we propose WaitLens, which fine-tunes a lightweight VLM on GUI loading trajectories annotated with waiting end times. We introduce temporal resampling to construct diverse training examples, enabling the model to learn varying GUI loading behaviors from temporal observations. Experiments show that WaitLens achieves 86.08% accuracy in deciding whether to keep waiting, compared with 54.13% for the backbone VLM. | **[BeforeIT.jl: High-Performance Agent-Based Macroeconomics Made Easy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.13267v2)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>BeforeIT is an open-source software for building and simulating state-of-the-art macroeconomic agent-based models (macro ABMs) based on the recently introduced macro ABM developed in [1] and here referred to as the base model. We present the main structure of the software, demonstrate its ease of use with illustrative examples, and benchmark its performance. Our benchmarks demonstrate that the base model built with BeforeIT is orders of magnitude faster than its original Matlab implementation, while achieving performance comparable to a highly optimised HPC implementation. Furthermore, we show how BeforeIT supports an easy construction of model variants such as the 'CANVAS' model of [2], how these variants can be evaluated at scale via an automated multicountry forecasting pipeline, and how the entire simulation can be automatically differentiated. BeforeIT is designed to facilitate reproducibility, extensibility, and experimentation. As the first open-source, industry-grade software to build macro ABMs, BeforeIT can significantly foster collaboration and innovation in the field of agent-based macroeconomic modelling. The package, along with its documentation, is freely available at https://github.com/bancaditalia/BeforeIT.jl under the Apache 2.0 license.</p></details> | 16 pages, 11 figures |
| **[CauProphet: Teaching Agents to Develop Causal Cognition like Humans](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32477v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM) agents increasingly support long-horizon tasks by leveraging external memory and past experience. For each task, CauProphet combines relevant knowledge in Causal Cognition with current evidence to construct a Causal Belief, a structured belief capturing causal factors and the effects of possible actions. It uses this belief for decision-making and continuously updates both the belief and Causal Cognition as new observations emerge, enabling causal knowledge that persists beyond individual experiences. Evaluations demonstrate that CauProphet progressively acquires causal knowledge and transfers it to future decision-making. In financial research, CauProphet leverages prior discoveries to improve downstream analyses and outperforms all baselines in mean rank correlation with next-day returns, achieving a 25.4% improvement over a baseline agent. It also improves prediction accuracy from 21.3% to 28.6% in dynamic real-world settings. Further experiments validate its ability to perform reliable causal analysis and action selection across scientific and everyday tasks, demonstrating the potential of causal experience learning for next-generation LLM agents.</p></details> | <details><summary>30 pa...</summary><p>30 pages, including appendices</p></details> |
| **[An LLM agent for end-to-end computational materials discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20434v2)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Coordinating multi-scale tasks is an effective strategy for computational materials discovery, yet the diverse algorithms and tools involved make this process highly challenging. Here we present DREAMS, a large language model (LLM)-based agent system capable of executing the entire screening pipeline for metal-organic frameworks (MOFs). DREAMS processes a large body of MOF literature, links publications to their crystal structures, and curates them into a computation-ready database, which is then screened with progressively more expensive simulations. | **[Agentic Memory Management Is the Next Frontier of Information Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32464v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Information retrieval has long been focusing on connecting people with information they need to complete their tasks. Recently, the meaning of this has changed dramatically as more and more AI agents now consume, retain, and act on information on a user's behalf. Consequently, information management now also takes place \emph{inside} the agent in the form of memory management. In this perspective paper, we argue that \emph{agentic memory management} (AMM), which studies the structure, analysis, organization, storage, searching, retrieval, and \emph{use} of information in an intelligent agent's memory, is a natural extension of IR research and an area in which the IR community can make distinctive contributions. Specifically, we first present a system-level framework for AMM and introduce a computational taxonomy of memory objects as \emph{constants}, \emph{functions}, and \emph{operators}, each with distinct retrieval semantics. Then, we compare AMM with established IR research and identify their difference and connections. Last but not least, we outline a research agenda covering benchmarks, candidate principles of memory utility, optimization procedures that mix parametric and non-parametric updates, and more. We hope this paper could attract more people, both inside and outside the IR communities, to think more about AMM problems and research from IR perspectives.</p></details> |  |

To address this limitation, we introduce Poseidon, an automated pipeline for generating diverse, verifiable training environments from scientific code repositories at scale. From the same repository budget, Poseidon extracts nearly 10 times as many training environments as a prior approach by decomposing scientific workflows into four complementary task types: Reproduction, Subflow, Completion, and Correction, where each type targets a distinct capability for scientific research. A recursive improvement loop further refines failed candidates and recovers otherwise discarded environments. Using Poseidon, we construct Pearl, a collection of 97,659 executable and verifiable environments from 7,110 public repositories spanning 10 scientific disciplines. Qwen3.5 models (9B, 27B, and 35B-A3B) trained on Pearl achieve consistent gains across six challenging scientific-agent benchmarks. However, these observations are subject to uncertainty and may be incorrect even when tools execute successfully. When agents accept incorrect observations, the errors can propagate through subsequent reasoning and cause task failure. We analyze 1,229 execution trajectories across three remote sensing agent benchmarks. On each benchmark, at least 88.1 percent of tasks depend on tool observations. Among these tasks, at least 22.7 percent contain incorrect observations despite successful tool execution. These errors propagate to the final answer in at least 82.0 percent of affected tasks on each benchmark. To address this problem, we propose RSure-Agent, a framework for verifying tool observations and limiting error propagation. We introduce a verifiable observation protocol that requires tools to return process evidence for the agent to verify their observations. We construct a task-tool reliability prior from offline task feedback. The prior summarizes each tool configuration's past performance across task types and provides a task-specific reference for verification. Using process evidence and this prior, RSure-Agent decides whether to accept an observation, request additional evidence, or reject it. We evaluate RSure-Agent on EarthBench, ThinkGeo, TerraLogic, and CHOICE-420. Across three agent benchmarks, RSure-Agent reduces the error propagation rate by 21.3 to 25.9 percentage points relative to the base configuration with verification and the prior disabled. On EarthBench, it reduces the tool-call ratio by 25.9 percent relative to Earth-Agent.</p></details> | <details><summary>The d...</summary><p>The demo is available at https://github.com/airs101/RSure-Agent (code will be released for further research)</p></details> |

We introduce Async-RBench, a benchmark for asynchronous task execution in LLM agents, with 200 executable tasks covering eight scenarios. A scenario controller regulates result delivery using predefined task-state conditions, while an Event Response Contract (ERC) specifies what each response must change, preserve, avoid, and re-verify. The Dynamic Replanning Score (DRS) weights the response process and outcome equally and is reported separately from task success. We evaluate nine agents and find DRS scores of 19.2 to 45.1, with the lowest scores on duplicate reports and subagent failures. Under our execution protocol, all nine agents obtain lower task success with asynchronous than with batched delivery (26.0% vs. 29.7% on average). For three agents, prompting the main agent to rebuild its plan after every result improves DRS by only 2.6 to 3.5 points. | **[Measuring the Energy Cost of Completing Tasks with Quantized Language-Model Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32417v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Quantization reduces the memory and energy needed to serve large language models (LLMs) and AI agents for coding and data analysis can now run on locally hosted LLMs. In practice, this benefit is commonly benchmarked per output token. Yet an agent calls its model repeatedly, runs tools, and revises its work, and quantization can change both how much the agent generates and whether it succeeds. Existing energy studies either compare quantized models on single requests or measure agents at one fixed precision, overlooking how a change in precision plays out across a complete agent task. To address this gap, this paper presents the first characterization of the task-level energy trade-offs of model quantization in locally hosted agents. We develop 29 agent tasks with automatic pass/fail checks in six families, ranging from single-answer SQL analytics to multi-step repository bug fixing, and measure CPU and GPU energy per output token and per completed task for 4-bit and 8-bit versions of two open-weight models on bare-metal NVIDIA A100 nodes with llama.cpp. Our findings reveal that 4-bit precision generally saves energy per token, though whether it saves energy per completed task is task dependent and changes with the model and the execution budget (i.e., limits on turns, tokens, and time). We trace this discrepancy to the extra tokens needed to complete a task in full, which translates into using up to 3.5 times as much energy per completed task. These findings offer actionable guidance for developers and operators choosing model precision for agents, showing how tokens per completed task, set against the per-token saving, reveal whether lower precision completes the work with less energy.</p></details> | 26 pages, 7 figures |
| **[Can LLM Annotators Match Human Consistency? A time frame from a coastal model can hold hundreds of megabytes, and if the archive's chunking does not suit the query, the agent will spend more time downloading and compacting than gaining insights. After all, the archives were designed for storage, not for the fast, on-the-fly access agents need. Speech and music once hit the same wall. A minute of audio is millions of numbers, yet today's audio models hold conversations and write music. They "learned" to do this by "listening" to tokens. Neural audio codecs turn waveforms into short sequences of discrete symbols that a model reads like words and decodes back into sound. Coastal water is a similar signal: dense, finely sampled, and repeatable. We are convinced it has an analogous compact native representation that preserves the salient physics. Tokens are that representation: small integers, light enough for an agent to sweep six months of a coastline in seconds. Agents can then do more than simple lookup-related tasks; they can form and test their hypotheses as they explore the tokenized representation efficiently. Herein, we build this framework, using vector quantized variational autoencoders to compress tide level, the non-tidal residual, and surface wind speed into 16x16 grids of tokens along the US Atlantic and Gulf coasts. We propose a general framework that connects data-driven behavioural discovery with analysis and experimental investigation. Categories developed from recorded activity define what is measured. Analyses preserve the behaviours' positions in the process and express their relationships as interpretable hypotheses. Statistical assessment and tests in held-out contexts examine the strength and scope of the evidence. Experiments, including prompt steering, then test how behaviour and outcomes change under deliberately varied conditions. A study of iterative self-reflection illustrates how discovery, dependency learning and prompt comparisons can inform one another. We develop this approach for trajectories of varying length, event order and agent roles, and identify advances in coding, analysis and experimentation needed to support its wider use.</p></details> | <details><summary>Under...</summary><p>Under review by Nature Machine Intelligence</p></details> |
| **[Flow-ERD: Agent-type Aware Flow Matching With Entropy-Regularized Distillation for Diverse Traffic Simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06957v2)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Realistic and diverse traffic simulation is essential to autonomous driving development. Yet prevailing benchmarks predominantly reward realism, and recent methods have optimized accordingly, leaving diversity underexplored. We introduce Flow-ERD, a multi-agent simulator that pursues realism and diversity jointly. Its backbone, Agent-Type Aware Flow Matching (AFM), couples flow matching's multi-modal expressiveness with type-specific kinematic execution. It preserves fine-grained diversity while keeping motions consistent with each agent type. A second stage, Entropy-Regularized Distillation (ERD), fine-tunes the closed-loop rollout distribution with an entropy-regularized reverse-KL objective. This mitigates closed-loop covariate shift while explicitly preventing collapse onto high-density modes. We evaluate Flow-ERD with Cross-Pair Diversity and its validity-gated variant alongside standard realism scores. Validation experiments further show that tuning entropy regularization recovers most of the pretrained backbone's diversity while retaining realism gains. Flow-ERD ranks first in test-split realism on the Waymo Open Sim Agents Challenge (WOSAC) and dominates the validation realism--diversity Pareto front among reproducible baselines.</p></details> | <details><summary>8 pag...</summary><p>8 pages, 5 figures, 2 tables. Accepted for publication in IEEE Robotics and Automation Letters (RA-L). Updated to the accepted author manuscript. Seulbin Hwang and Kiyoung Om are co-first authors; Jinhan Lee is the corresponding author. Project page and code updates: https://seulbinhwang.github.io/flow-erd-project-page/</p></details> |
| **[Understanding Communication Failures in Multi-Agent LLM Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32060v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multi-agent LLM systems aim to improve reasoning through debate, evidence sharing, and mutual correction, but interaction can also introduce failures that final accuracy alone cannot explain. Agents may converge too early, ignore dissent, suppress correct minority answers, or preserve a wrong framing across rounds. We present a corpus-grounded taxonomy of communication failures, built from 3,211 failed multi-round trajectories across medical QA, coding, finance, multi-hop reasoning, ethics, and embodied text tasks. Using this taxonomy, we analyze failure distributions across datasets and system designs, round-level error dynamics, recovery hardness, majority transitions, minority-signal suppression, and self-reported confidence. We find that failures are dominated by recovery/correction and consensus/decision problems rather than isolated first-round mistakes, with many emerging after agents first observe peer responses. Based on these findings, we recommend stronger disagreement handling, evidence verification, minority-signal preservation, and recovery checks before finalization.</p></details> |  |
| **[NeuroMAS: Multi-Agent Systems as Neural Networks with Joint Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.16757v2)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multi-agent language systems are often built as hand-designed workflows, where agents are assigned semantic roles and communication protocols are specified in advance. We propose NeuroMAS, a method that first treats a multi-agent language system as a trainable and scalable neural-network-like architecture with LLM agents as nodes and intermediate textual signals as edges. In NeuroMAS, agent nodes are role-free but structure-aware: the topology only determines how information can flow in general, while reinforcement learning training determines how nodes communicate, specialize, and coordinate. This formulation shifts multi-agent design from workflow engineering toward architecture design, where depth, width, connectivity, and growth protocol become scalable sources of capability. Further, we provide a theoretical perspective showing why such modular textual computation is more parameter-efficient when tasks admit hierarchical decompositions. Experiments show that NeuroMAS improves significantly over both inference-time and trained multi-agent baselines. We further find that organizational scaling is path-dependent: larger systems can be challenging to train from scratch, but become feasible when grown progressively from smaller trained systems. These results suggest that learned neural multi-agent systems are a promising scaling axis for LLMs.</p></details> |  |
| **[Do AI-for-Science Agents Preserve Model Safety?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32045v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>AI-for-Science agents are increasingly being used for formulating scientific hypotheses, running autonomous experiments, and interpreting evidence. These agents are constructed by integrating a large language model (LLM) into a domain-specific agent harness, which in turn can improve the LLM's scientific capability and enable the model to orchestrate scientific tools. In this work, we ask whether model safety is preserved when an LLM is integrated in an AI-for-Science agent harness. We evaluate five agent harnesses on 50 harmful chemistry requests. Specifically, we study how the safety behavior changes under direct prompting, two simple adversarial prompting strategies, and an adaptive attack. Across four recent GPT variants, our results show that the agent harness can increase harmful compliance even under direct prompting. More critically, we find that adaptive attacks that are largely unsuccessful against the base LLM suddenly become highly successful after the agent harness is introduced. Our results show that the safety behavior of the base LLM is not durable once a harness is introduced. Thus, as AI-for-Science agents become more capable and autonomous, the community needs to evaluate and align the agent using domain-specific safety benchmarks.</p></details> |  |
| **[RSI-Opt: Recursive Self-Improvement Optimizer Agents For Model Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32026v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Conventional neural-network training typically follows a predefined optimization recipe, motivating a LLM agent that monitors training dynamics and adjusts optimizer settings as training progresses, but a fixed agent policy does not accumulate experience across runs. We introduce RSI-Opt, a plug-and-play Recursive Self-Improvement Optimizer Agents which evolves the knowledge that guides this process: a prompt, a playbook of procedures, a library of tested optimizer code, and a decision cadence. A meta-agent rewrites this policy using evidence from earlier runs, and an archive retains evaluated policies for further evolution. Across 5 generations on three training tasks, the meta-agents develop reusable procedures and code and conduct their own CPU proxy experiments. RSI-Opt substantially improves optimizer-agent performance, with representative raw-space summary $z$-scores increasing from $+10.48$ to $+14.33$ on the evolution suite with Opus 5.5 and from $-0.36$ to $+3.11$ on held-out tasks with GPT-5.5 through cross-model policy transfer. On the Burgers PDE, it reduces mean relative $L_2$ error by approximately 2.7-fold with Opus 5.5 and 8.8-fold with GPT-6.1 over the fixed-prompt agent baseline, although PDE transfer varies across LLMs and tasks. Controlled comparisons show that the evolved policy combines reusable optimization knowledge with adaptation to each task: initial decisions capture most observed gains on seen tasks, while continued adaptation improves outcomes on unseen tasks. These findings demonstrate how procedures and executable tools accumulated across training runs can improve optimizer-agent performance and support transfer to scientific machine learning.</p></details> |  |
| **[From Review to Authorization: Key-Isolated Threshold Signing for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05901v2)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Autonomous LLM agents can turn semantic judgments into consequential actions such as payments and permission changes. If the same process makes these judgments and controls a reusable signing credential, a mistaken judgment can reach execution without further approval. To make LLM review govern execution, we present KITA, an authorization architecture that keeps the user's personal signing key and every threshold signing-key share outside all LLM processes. Under threshold-signature unforgeability and our system assumptions, an adversary controlling the proposer and fewer than t reviewer-signer domains cannot produce a valid authorization for a new action without signing contributions from t distinct domains. This execution-bound authorization integrity ensures that each accepted authorization includes a share from an uncompromised domain, bound to the canonical action and released only after authenticated reviewer approval. We implement an in-process prototype of KITA with a structured-output LLM adapter and threshold BLS. Six system tests validate quorum gating and message binding, seven controlled baseline scenarios compare authorization outcomes, and cryptographic microbenchmarks measure online signing cost and its scaling behavior.</p></details> | 18 pages |
| **[CRM-Agent-Bench: Benchmarking Tool-Using Agents in Stateful Enterprise Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32004v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Deploying language agents in real applications demands more than the right answer: an agent must execute a multi-step business process correctly, follow domain-specific rules, and interact with a user across a dialogue, where the gap between saying and doing is the gap between a satisfied customer and a real loss. We argue that a deployable-agent benchmark should jointly assess four facets: the outcome an agent reaches, the trajectory it takes, its operational viability, and its policy compliance. Customer relationship management (CRM), where mutable records meet operational rules and consequential effects, exercises all four. We introduce CRM-Agent-Bench, an execution-grounded benchmark of 555 stateful, multi-turn tasks across nine business domains and 36 capability tags. Each task supplies a task-specific executable contract drawn from checks over actions, arguments, order, response, target state, and protected state; success is the strict conjunction of the checks declared for that task. We evaluate the complete agent system through these contracts and report scored coverage, latency, and cost. Across sixteen systems evaluated on the audited final release, strict success spans 64.32% to 83.60%; the closely grouped leading systems nonetheless diverge sharply in cost and per-domain reliability. CRM-Agent-Bench offers an auditable framework for measuring contract-grounded business-transaction integrity and adherence to task-declared benchmark policies, with a portable structure designed for extension as models improve. Github repository: https://github.com/salesforce-misc/crm-agent-bench</p></details> | 52 pages |
| **[DraMaze: A Multi-Agent Pipeline with Future-Aware Memory for Diverse Microdrama Script Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32002v1)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The rapid growth of microdramas has increased demand for high-quality script production. Long-form microdrama script generation with LLMs requires both diverse plot progression and long-range narrative coherence. Our observations reveal that candidate continuations increasingly concentrate on fewer narrative mechanisms as generation progresses, while conventional retrieval may reinforce trope reuse and overlook historical information essential to future plots. To address these challenges, we propose DraMaze, a multi-agent pipeline that explores diverse causal paths through trope memory-guided evolutionary tree search and mutation. It further constructs a Backward Narrative Dependency Tree to organize, retain, and retrieve historical memories based on future narrative goals. We also introduce MiDramaBench, a bilingual benchmark for evaluating script logic and creative quality across different script lengths, together with causal-pattern metrics for narrative repetition and diversity. Experiments show consistent improvements across three backbone LLMs. For 60-episode scripts of up to 60k words, DraMaze reduces causal-pattern repetition by 32.1% and achieves a Logic score of 82.5, outperforming the best-performing baseline by 8.5 points.</p></details> |  |
